    SettingsService#

    class · public with sharing

    implements ISettingsService

    setup/service/SettingsService.cls

    Docs note

    Two distinct API surfaces live in this one class. ISettingsService (getSettings/ saveSettings/detectAndUpdateOrgFeatures) is the instance-based CRUD path used only by SetupController for the admin settings screen. Everything else — the large block of static typed accessors below (logLevel, refundsEnabled, retryIntervalDays, donorPortalShowSummary, and so on) — is the canonical, sanctioned way every OTHER class in the package reads a single Settings__c value; callers must never call Settings__c.getInstance() directly or branch on a raw flag. Each accessor has a matching @TestVisible *Override static field so tests can stub one setting without inserting or upserting a Settings__c row.

    Two recurring patterns worth knowing before adding a new accessor: (1) capability gates (personAccountEnabled, npspEnabled, npspGauSyncEnabled, npspRecurringSyncEnabled) are always "detected AND admin-enabled" — detection is authoritative and an admin flag can only opt OUT of a capability the org actually supports, never opt into one it doesn't; (2) several Boolean fields are stored inverted as Hide_/Disabled__c (donorPortalShow, refundsEnabled, isEmbedEnabled) specifically so that a Checkbox field added in a later release, which backfills existing Settings__c rows to false, defaults to the "on"/visible behavior with no data migration required.

    Reads via getOrgDefaults(), never getInstance(). Settings__c has no profile/user tier — every accessor in this file treats it as an org-wide singleton — but getInstance() still resolves the hierarchy for the RUNNING USER.

    When the org-default row exists but the running context doesn't cleanly resolve to it, upserting getInstance()'s result can create a stray row scoped to that user, which then permanently shadows the real org-default for every later getInstance() call by that user — the exact bug already fixed for recordTypeMappingsRaw()/saveRecordTypeMappingsRaw() below, extended here to the shared getSettings()/saveSettings() CRUD path (SetupController's save handlers, and the org-logo methods: ensureDefaultLogoSeeded()/uploadOrgLogo()/removeOrgLogo()).

    Reproduced live on pledgivo-rtm-verify-19793: an admin's logo upload landed on a shadow row the guest-facing UiConfigController never reads, so the uploaded logo silently never appeared on donor pages.

    ContentVersion/ContentDistribution are file-handling utility objects, not part of any fflib-registered SObject graph — same rationale as the Settings__c direct-upsert exception above (and AppLogger's direct Log__c insert): routing one-off file plumbing through the UnitOfWork would add nothing but ceremony. DML/SOQL here are deliberate.

    They ship as packaged static resources and are served from the org's own <org>.file.force.com domain, which a guest LWR site allows in img-src with no CSP Trusted Site. That is the whole reason for the ContentDistribution round trip: a remotely-hosted stock photo would need a packaged CspTrustedSite, and every packaged trusted site shows up in the install-time "Approve Third-Party Access" dialog — which this package deliberately keeps to Stripe only.

    WITHOUT SHARING, deliberately and narrowly. Seeding runs from PostInstallHandler, which executes as a per-package system user, and in that context a with sharing class cannot resolve StaticResource at all — the query dies with "sObject type 'StaticResource' is not supported" and the whole install used to abort there. Found 2026-08-09 by install-testing into a real subscriber org; it is invisible in a dev org, where an admin can read the resource in any sharing mode.

    This class is the ONLY part of logo handling that runs without sharing, and it only ever touches the package's own packaged resources plus the documents it creates from them. Admin- and donor-supplied images stay on the USER_MODE path in uploadOrgLogo() — do not route them through here.

    CURRENCY IS NOT A SETTING AND NO LONGER LIVES HERE. defaultCurrency() and currencyCodeForGateway() used to read Settings__c.Default_Currency__c; that field is deleted and the answer now comes from the org itself, via CurrencyResolver.forOrg() / forCampaign() / forGateway(). Do not reintroduce an accessor here — a second name for the same answer is exactly the ambiguity that let an INR org charge in USD.

    Describe result is cached by the platform per transaction, so the wizard pays this once.

    Enforced in two places, and it needs both. CampaignService masks Allow_Tribute__c to false on every campaign payload it serves, which is what actually hides the section; and DonationService.applyTributeAndMatch refuses to capture tribute fields off the staging options blob, which is what stops a hand-crafted guest submission writing them anyway. The first is the product behavior, the second is the enforcement — a client-side gate alone would be advisory on a public endpoint.

    No separate send-time gate in ReceiptService: with capture blocked the Opportunity has no Tribute_Type__c, so buildTributeNotification's existing gates already return null. An in-flight staging row created while the switch was on is finalized through the same capture path, so flipping the switch off stops its e-card too.

    Same field, read for a different purpose: the starting value of a brand-new Campaign's Allow_Tribute__c, applied by FundraiserAdminController.getFundraiserOptions() for the "New fundraiser" wizard (lexFundraisersConsole). Deliberately the same answer as tributeEnabled() — offering the wizard a toggle that defaults to on while the org switch suppresses it everywhere would let an admin create a fundraiser whose tribute section silently never renders.

    Four knobs rather than two because a limit without its window is meaningless, and an admin tuning this is reacting to something concrete ("we are seeing 40 attempts an hour from one address") that needs both halves to express. Every fallback below is repeated verbatim in setCheckoutProtection.html's help text, per the house style every other numeric settings panel follows — change a number here and change it there.

    This is only the donor-facing ask. It grants nothing on its own — a request parks at Awaiting Review for a person, and refundsEnabled()/refundWindowDays() still bound whether the refund itself can be issued afterwards.

    Turning this on is only half the job — inline playback also needs a CSP Trusted Site for https://www.youtube-nocookie.com that the admin creates themselves. This package ships no trusted site of its own (see the posture note above createLogoDistribution), so the flag is an intent switch, not a capability check. With it on and no trusted site, the browser blocks the frame; the setup panel therefore states the requirement next to the toggle.

    The cadence of the two sub-hourly job families lives here rather than only in CronTrigger, because CronTrigger is not a place a cadence can be stored. Salesforce has no sub-hourly recurrence, so each family is one job per minute-offset within the hour, and the interval is only implied by how many of them exist. That implication is destroyed by the very method that keeps the pipeline healthy: PostInstallHandler.scheduleJobs() fills in every offset the packaged interval expects, so an admin who chose 15 minutes had the missing 5-minute offsets silently re-added on the next upgrade, Health-Check Fix, or settings save — and until then expectedJobCount() graded their org against 12 finalizer entries and reported a permanent shortfall for a setting the console itself offered. Audit finding F-119.

    The interval is also a slot budget and a donor-visible latency, which is why the allowed set is closed rather than free-form: 60/interval scheduled-Apex jobs out of the org's hard limit of 100, and up to one interval between a card being charged and the Opportunity existing (see apex-safety, "Only the schedulers finalize").

    An out-of-range stored value falls back to the packaged default rather than throwing or being used as-is. The field is admin-writable through the standard Custom Settings UI as well as the console panel, and a hand-typed 7 would otherwise produce entries at :00 :07 … :56 — a family that does not tile the hour, whose last gap is 4 minutes and whose implied interval no longer matches anything the panel can display. Falling back keeps the pipeline running on a known-good cadence instead.

    The four fallbacks below (3 retries, 3-day interval, 7-day grace, 3-day dunning throttle) are also quoted verbatim in setRecurring.html's help text, per the house style every other settings panel follows — an optional numeric field states its fallback in the same sentence. Until 2026-08-20 (F-98) this was the one panel that did not, so four empty boxes governing how a failed recurring gift is chased read as unconfigured and invited an admin to replace working defaults with a guess. Change a number here and change it there.

    Answers "is the job that turns confirmed gifts into Opportunities actually running?" — a question a CronTrigger count cannot answer, because a job can be scheduled and still abort on every fire. Settings__c.Finalizer_Heartbeat__c is written from INSIDE StagingReconciliationBatch.finish(), so it only advances when a pass genuinely completed. Callers: the guest donation path (DonationService.createDonorAndIntent, which alarms but never refuses a gift on staleness alone), the setup console (SetupController's health check and the "Start the background jobs" Get Started step), and the checkout gate below — which reads the same field for the stricter "has NEVER completed a pass" question and does refuse.

    DERIVED from the configured cadence, not a constant — and it has to be, since F-119 made the finalizer interval an admin setting (5..60 minutes). A fixed 30-minute threshold is SHORTER THAN A SINGLE PASS at the longest cadence: an org finalizing hourly would report stale for half of every hour by design, hold Get Started step 6 permanently red, turn the Gifts Being Recorded check red, and write a PIPELINE_STALE Transaction_Log__c against every gift taken in that window — an alarm crying wolf on a healthy org, which is how the next real one gets ignored.

    So the alarm waits three missed passes and never less than the floor. At the default 5-minute cadence that is max(30, 15) = 30, exactly what it was before F-119; at 60 minutes it is 180. Raising the interval buys slower detection — that is the trade the admin is making, and the Settings console says so next to the control.

    A HARDER question than isFinalizerPipelineStale above, and the two must not be confused. Staleness is transient — a healthy org whose jobs paused for half an hour is stale and recovers on its own, which is why staleness only raises the PIPELINE_STALE alarm and never refuses a gift. A NULL heartbeat is different in kind: no pass has EVER completed in this org, so nothing has ever demonstrated that a charged card can become an Opportunity. That is the exact state a fresh install sits in until an admin re-owns the background jobs, and the state the 1.0.0.24 install audit found a real card being charged in — the donor's money moved and the Salesforce record never appeared, with nothing red anywhere in the UI. The sweep does eventually recover such a gift, but a subscriber should not be able to reach that state at all. Audit finding §4b.

    HOW LONG A FRESH INSTALL SITS BLOCKED: until an admin acts, not one cadence. PostInstallHandler.scheduleJobs() does schedule the whole pipeline during install, and every cron entry it creates sits at WAITING on the right cadence (24 of them at the packaged defaults; fewer once an admin widens a cadence) — but they are owned by the post-install system user, and CronTrigger.OwnerId never changes. That owner holds no permission set, so every pass throws inside start() before it reads a row and finish() never runs, which is the method that stamps the heartbeat. Measured on a virgin 1.0.0.26 org: ten consecutive batch runs, zero successes, heartbeat still null after half an hour; re-owned, the very next pass completed and stamped it. The gate opens when the jobs are re-owned — Get Started step 6, the Health Check Fix button, or any SetupController.saveSettings() call, all of which route to PostInstallHandler.scheduleJobs(true) — and the first pass after that stamps it. See F-67 on scheduleJobs(Boolean) for why the install-time owner cannot work.

    Once the jobs are re-owned it clears within one cadence: StagingReconciliationBatch.finish() stamps the heartbeat unconditionally, whether or not the pass had any rows to reconcile, so the gate opens on the first completed pass after the jobs are (re-)scheduled under a reachable owner.

    Arms the real heartbeat read inside a test. Every test context has a null heartbeat by construction — no batch has run — so an unguarded gate would refuse every existing guest-checkout test for a reason none of them is about. Rather than weaken the gate, it stays shut off under Test.isRunningTest() unless a test explicitly arms it, which is how the gate's own tests exercise both real branches.

    This is the outer of the two bounds on a signed-in session: the sliding idle window (Portal_Idle_Timeout_Minutes__c) ends a session nobody is using, and this ends one that has been used continuously, no matter how active the donor is. It is not the lifetime of anything a donor is emailed — the emailed sign-in code has its own, much shorter, TTL in Portal_Code_TTL_Minutes__c. The session token itself never leaves the browser tab's sessionStorage, so unlike the retired magic link there is nothing sitting in an inbox for this window to protect; the cap exists so a shared or unattended machine cannot hold a session open indefinitely. Admins who need longer raise Portal_Token_TTL_Hours__c; nothing hard-codes the number except this line, because every surface that states it reads it back through this accessor.

    The sign-in code's redeemable life. Clamped so an admin cannot stretch the weaker,

    Sliding idle window on a live portal session.

    Org-wide ceiling on sign-in codes issued in a rolling hour, read by GuestThrottleService.isPortalCodeRequestAllowed. Unclamped, unlike the three portal values above: those bound how long a credential lives, where a careless number is a security decision the package should not let an admin make. This one only bounds how much of the org's own email allocation the public sign-in form may spend, so the admin is the right authority — including zero, which turns the limit off the way every other throttle ceiling in this class does.

    Three defaults, not one, because the three cases make mutually exclusive factual claims — see the doc comment on the Receipt_Legal_Text_With_Goods__c field for why the single override this replaced was a correctness bug. These stay textually in step with the LEGAL_* constants in ReceiptStatusHelper, which are the last-resort fallback for an org that clears a seeded field; PostInstallHandler seeds all three from here.

    This is the ONLY way to build a donor-facing link from internal (Lightning) context. Site.getBaseUrl() returns blank off-site and URL.getOrgDomainUrl() returns the internal My Domain host, which a guest cannot open — so an internal sender that falls back to the org domain silently emails a dead link. Prefer Site.getBaseUrl() when running inside the site (it is the host the visitor actually arrived on) and this accessor everywhere else.

    Single source of truth for the assembled link. Campaign.Public_URL__c stores only the slug, and the Fundraisers list's "Copy link" button handed that bare slug to the clipboard while reporting success — the admin found out from whoever they sent it to (audit finding F-61). FundraiserDetailController already carried a private copy of this assembly for the record view; it now delegates here so the list row and the record view cannot drift.

    The address donor email is sent FROM. Consumed only by EmailSenderService, which turns it into an OrgWideEmailAddress id — Apex cannot invent a From address, so a value here that does not match a verified, all-profiles org-wide address has no effect at all.

    THE ONLY DEFAULT-ON FLAG ON Settings__c THAT IS STORED POSITIVELY, and it needs the getOrgDefaults()/Id dance below to stay default-on. A field's <defaultValue> is applied when the platform CREATES a record; it is NOT applied to the empty instance a custom setting getter synthesises when no record exists. An unstored Checkbox reads back false, so <defaultValue>true</defaultValue> on Auto_Receipt_Enabled__c buys nothing at read time — this used to return FALSE on any org where the post-install seed had not run, which is exactly the org that can least afford receipts to be off, and this package has already had a post-install seed silently fail once.

    getInstance() cannot express the fix: its Id is null whether or not a row exists (verified in-org 2026-08-23 — it returns a merged synthetic instance), so it cannot tell "never configured" from "admin unticked the box". getOrgDefaults() returns the actual org-default record, Id and all, so a null Id there means nothing has ever been stored. Reading org defaults loses nothing: Settings__c has no profile/user tier (see this class's header), which is why several accessors already read that way.

    The other default-ON switches — Refunds_Disabled__c, Embed_Disabled__c — dodge all of this by being stored INVERTED, so an unset checkbox already means "enabled". That is the pattern to copy for a new default-on flag; this one is the exception, not the model.

    The three org-wide fallbacks behind the public page's contact band. Each returns null when unset so the band can omit the line entirely rather than print an empty label — a contact block showing "Phone:" with nothing after it reads as broken, which is the opposite of the trust the band exists to build. A campaign's own Contact_*_Override__c wins over these; CampaignService resolves that precedence, not this class.

    Reads Support_Phone__c rather than a contact-specific field — an org has one support number, and the payment-failure emails and the public contact band should never be able to disagree about what it is.

    These seven describe the ORGANIZATION, not a theme. They lived on Campaign_Design__c until 2026-08-12, where a campaign switching from one packaged theme to another would have silently changed which privacy policy its donors were pointed at. Each returns null when unset so the footer can omit the line or the icon entirely — a dead "Terms" link or a social icon that opens nothing is worse for trust than no link at all, which is the whole point of the band they render in. A campaign's own *_Override__c wins over these; CampaignService.resolveIdentity owns that precedence, not this class.

    Deliberately a SECOND product rather than reusing the donation add-on one: both lines are fully deductible and both would sum correctly, but a covered fee is not an extra gift, and sharing the product would inflate every add-on total an admin reports on by the fees.

    RecordTypeMappingService owns the delimited-encoding format (what the string MEANS); this class only owns the mechanics of splitting/joining that string across the 4 physical Settings__c chunk fields (WHERE it's stored). Replaces the former Record_Type_Mapping__c custom object (deleted 2026-08-03) — Custom Settings fields cap at 255 chars with no Long Text Area option, so the encoded table is chunked here instead of living in a dedicated Long-Text-capable object.

    Reads via getOrgDefaults(), never getInstance() — record type mapping config is an org-wide singleton with no profile/user tier, and getInstance()'s hierarchy resolution caches on first call for the rest of the transaction, which previously made this read (when it ran first) silently freeze OTHER getInstance()-based accessors (e.g. eventDeductibleMode()) against later same-transaction Settings__c DML — a real Apex custom-settings platform gotcha, not just a test artifact.

    AppLogger#

    class · public without sharing

    shared/AppLogger.cls

    Docs note

    Two deliberate gotchas for anyone modifying this class: (1) without sharing + a direct insert (not Application.UnitOfWork) is required because a log call can happen from inside a UoW commit's own catch block — routing through the UoW here would risk a circular/nested commit; (2) the DML-limit guard in write() exists because cacheable @AuraEnabled methods run with a DML limit of 0 — without that guard, logging a caught exception from a cacheable method would itself throw a LimitException and mask the original error the log call was trying to capture.

    Application#

    class · public inherited sharing

    shared/Application.cls

    Docs note

    The UnitOfWork registration order below is not cosmetic — fflib's UnitOfWork inserts SObjectTypes in list order, so a parent must be registered before any child whose lookup will be resolved via registerRelationship() in the same commit (see the inline comments beside each entry for the specific FK it exists to satisfy). Adding a new object here means finding its true parents in this list, not appending to the end.

    IPaymentGateway is deliberately NOT in the Service map below — PaymentGatewayFactory resolves it dynamically per Payment_Account__c record instead of a single static binding, since an org can have more than one active gateway account. Which one a given campaign charges against is Campaign.Payment_Account__c.

    AttributionCapture#

    class · public with sharing

    shared/AttributionCapture.cls

    Turns the raw query string of a checkout URL into the attribution fields carried by a donation or a recurring gift. Pure utility — no SOQL, no DML, no callouts; it only filters, normalizes and writes onto an in-memory SObject, so the calling service keeps ownership of the Unit of Work.

    Usage: Map<String, Object> options = (Map<String, Object>) JSON.deserializeUntyped(staging.Options_JSON__c); AttributionCapture.apply(opp, options.get('attribution')); AttributionCapture.copy(recurringDonation, renewalOpportunity);

    Docs note

    Two caps guard the LongTextArea and the org's storage: at most MAX_KEYS parameters survive, each value truncated to MAX_VALUE_LENGTH, and the serialized JSON is dropped whole if it would still exceed MAX_JSON_LENGTH. A donor can put an unbounded query string on a public URL, so none of these are optional.

    EXCLUDED_KEYS is the functional/PII denylist — everything else on the URL is kept. The first group is how the public pages carry state (slug/amount/email/name/ref/ tickets) and would be noise in a marketing report; the second is Stripe's own redirect params and our receipt/dashboard tokens, which are credentials, not attribution, and must never be persisted onto a reportable record.

    The five UTM keys get their own reportable Text(255) columns; everything else that survives the denylist lands in URL_Parameters__c as JSON. Keys here are matched case-insensitively, so ?UTM_Source=Email fills UTM_Source__c just like ?utm_source=email.

    Method Returns
    apply(2 args) void
    calls: AppLogger.warn()
    copy(2 args) void
    calls: AppLogger.warn()
    normalize(1 arg) Map<String, String>

    ControllerAction#

    class · public abstract inherited sharing

    shared/ControllerAction.cls

    ControllerAction — base class for all LWC controller inner action classes.

    Usage:

      public class MyController {
      @AuraEnabled
      public static Map<String, Object> doSomething(String param) {
          return new DoSomethingAction(param).run();
      }

      private class DoSomethingAction extends ControllerAction {
          private String param;
          DoSomethingAction(String param) { this.param = param; }

          protected override void doValidate() {
              if (String.isBlank(param)) addError('param is required');
          }
          protected override Object doExecute() {
              return MyService.doWork(param);
          }
      }
  }

    Docs note

    This is what produces the { success, data/error } response envelope every @AuraEnabled method in this package must return (see AGENTS.md). A controller method that builds its response map by hand instead of extending this class is the one place that contract can silently drift.

    Every payload leaving an @AuraEnabled method is walked here: any SObject in it becomes a plain Map keyed by field API names, with the packaging namespace removed.

    Why it exists. An SObject handed to the client serializes under its runtime API names, so the same Campaign arrives as {"Tagline__c": ...} in an unmanaged dev org and as {"pledgivo__Tagline__c": ...} once the package carries a namespace — while every LWC in this repo reads the unprefixed name (source never carries the prefix; see the Namespace Rule in AGENTS.md). Verified 2026-08-09 in the pledgivo_test org:

    JSON.serialize(new Campaign(Header_Image_URL__c='x'))
→ {"attributes":{...},"pledgivo_test__Header_Image_URL__c":"x"}

    The failure is silent — no error, every read is simply undefined — and it degrades into data loss wherever a panel builds its save payload from what it just read, which is what the settings console does: blank reads were saved back over real values.

    Why here rather than per payload. run() is the single choke point every controller already passes through to build the { success, data } envelope, so one implementation covers ~30 existing payloads and every future one. Hand-mapping each payload instead leaves the next new controller free to reintroduce the bug silently.

    Two things this does not do:

    • An Apex wrapper class holding an SObject in an @AuraEnabled field is serialized by the platform, not by this walk — so a wrapper must expose primitives, not records.
    • In an unmanaged org the prefix is empty, and this becomes a shape-only pass (SObject → Map).

    The branch order below is load-bearing — do not reorder. A list of SObjects reports instanceof SObject == true in Apex, so testing SObject first swallows every list and then fails the cast with "Invalid conversion from runtime type List<Campaign> to SObject", breaking every selectAll()-style controller in the package. List<SObject> must be tested first. The generic List<Object> branch then covers List<Map<String, Object>> and List<String> alike, since each element is re-walked.

    A payload holding no SObject is returned as-is, by identity, rather than rebuilt into an equivalent structure. That is not an optimization; it keeps the walk's runtime-type footprint down to the conversion it actually has to make:

    • Without the guard, the generic List<Object> branch rebuilds every list, so a List<String>, a List<RecordTypeMapping> or a List<GivingStatementService.StatementYear> comes back typed List<Object>.
    • The client cannot tell (identical JSON), but any Apex caller assigning the result to its declared type throws "Invalid conversion from runtime type List<ANY>".
    • The regression was real: 8 tests across four controllers failed in the 2026-08-10 package build, and non-test Apex calling a controller directly would have hit it too.

    With the guard, the only types this method changes are the ones it must: an SObject becomes a Map, a List<SObject> becomes a List<Object> of Maps, and any list or map containing one of those is rebuilt to carry the converted children.

    Answers "is there an SObject anywhere in this payload" — i.e. does stripNamespace have any work to do at all. It short-circuits on the first record found, so the common primitive-only payload costs one shallow pass and is then handed back untouched. The first test deliberately covers BOTH a record and a list of records: per the measured table above, List<Campaign> instanceof SObject is true, so no separate List<SObject> case is needed here (unlike in stripNamespace, where the two must be handled apart because only one of them is castable).

    Only POPULATED fields are emitted — the same set the platform would have serialized — so this neither adds nulls the client did not have before nor hides a queried field. Parent lookups (Campaign__r) and subquery children come back as SObject / List<SObject> and are walked recursively, so a nested record is stripped exactly like a top-level one.

    Derived from a describe at runtime, never hardcoded: correct with no edit in an unmanaged scratch org (empty prefix) and under the pledgivo namespace — it needed no change for the 2026-09-01 swap. A namespaced object name carries two "" (ns__Settings__c); a bare one carries a single "".

    Object-level delete permission has to be asserted HERE, by hand, because nothing downstream does it: fflib's UnitOfWork commits at AccessLevel.SYSTEM_MODE, and a config object with no trigger has no Domain layer to re-assert CRUD either. So an action that registerDeleted()s a record deletes it for any user who can reach the controller class, regardless of what the permission sets say. Both packaged deletes hit that: the three non-admin permission sets all carry allowDelete=false on Payment_Account__c and Campaign_Design__c, and neither was enforced before this guard. Read/field access is a different matter and stays with the selectors — this is only the delete gate.

    Method Returns
    run(0 args) Map<String, Object>
    calls: AppLogger.error()

    CurrencyResolver#

    class · public inherited sharing

    shared/CurrencyResolver.cls

    Decides the single currency a gift is charged, recorded and displayed in.

    Stateless helper beside CurrencyStampHelper, and the only place in the package that answers "what currency is this?". CurrencyStampHelper writes the answer onto a record; this class produces it.

    Docs note

    WHY THIS EXISTS. The currency used to come from Settings__c.Default_Currency__c, an admin-configurable text field that SetupController seeded with 'USD' at install. An admin who never opened Settings → General → Organization got USD forever — so an org running on INR displayed a rupee total on the Opportunity, a dollar sign on the receipt, and charged the donor in dollars. The setting had no legitimate use: a nonprofit's Salesforce org runs on one currency and its gateway settles in one currency, and an org-wide "charge in something else" toggle serves nobody. The field, the F-91 mismatch banner and the F-105 currency health check were all deleted with it — they existed only to detect a misconfiguration the package created for itself.

    The org's own currency cannot be wrong, so the failure mode is now structurally impossible rather than merely detected.

    PHASE 1 vs PHASE 2. forCampaign() returns the org answer today. It exists so that per-campaign currency in a multi-currency org (an org running a EUR appeal alongside an INR one) becomes a change to THIS class and nothing else — every caller is already routed through it. It takes the Campaign RECORD, not an Id, because fflib_SObjectSelector auto-selects CurrencyIsoCode in a multi-currency org, so the value Phase 2 needs is already on any row a selector returned. Taking an Id would force a query Phase 2 does not need.

    Test seam, matching SettingsService's *Override convention. UserInfo.getDefaultCurrency() and the CurrencyType table are org facts that cannot be stubbed, and no scratch org is both single- and multi-currency, so the resolved answer is overridable.

    Per-transaction cache. Resolution costs at most one SOQL (multi-currency orgs only); without the cache a donation request would spend one per call site. @TestVisible so a test can clear it between scenarios.

    No Phase 1 caller — deliberate, not dead code. The two charge paths (DonationFormController.PreparePayment, EventRegistrationController) use the org-scoped forGateway() only because no Campaign record is in scope on either; both switch to this overload the moment Phase 2 threads one through. Kept so that switch is a one-word edit rather than a new method, and covered by CurrencyResolver_Test either way.

    In a MULTI-currency org the answer is the CORPORATE currency, not UserInfo.getDefaultCurrency(). getDefaultCurrency() returns the RUNNING USER's personal currency there, which on a public donation page is the site's Guest User — a value an admin can change without ever thinking about donations. In a SINGLE-currency org getDefaultCurrency() is documented to return the organization's currency code, and it is the only currency the org can store, so it cannot be wrong.

    Organization.DefaultCurrencyIsoCode was considered and rejected: the column does not exist in a single-currency org (verified — the query fails with INVALID_FIELD), so it carries the same install-breaking compile hazard as CurrencyIsoCode.

    Split out from resolveOrgCurrency so the precedence rule is a pure function of two strings. Neither input can be stubbed — one is the CurrencyType table, the other is a UserInfo call, and no scratch org is both single- and multi-currency — so keeping the decision separate from the two org facts is what makes it directly testable in either.

    WHY THIS EXISTS. A SOQL aggregate over a Currency field ALWAYS returns the org's CORPORATE currency, and convertCurrency() cannot be applied to an aggregate result — both are stated outright in the SOQL and SOSL Reference (convertCurrency()): "If a query includes a GROUP BY or HAVING clause, currency data returned by using an aggregate function ... is in the org's default currency" and "You can't convert the result of an aggregate function into the user's currency".

    So every rollup that writes SUM(Amount) onto a parent record is writing a corporate figure into a field the platform will render in the PARENT's CurrencyIsoCode. On a campaign whose currency differs from corporate, Amount_Raised__c was labelled with the wrong denomination — and Campaign.Goal_Amount__c (already in the record's own currency) was being compared against it. The aggregate cannot be made to return anything else, so the conversion belongs here: after the aggregate, before the write.

    Nothing changes in a single-currency org. ratesByIso() returns an empty map there, so toRecordCurrency() hands back the amount it was given.

    Test seam, matching CurrencyStampHelper's field of the same name. UserInfo.isMultiCurrencyOrganization() cannot be stubbed and no scratch org is both single- and multi-currency, so the environment fact is overridable. The overrides only ever open a code path — every field read below is still guarded by its own try/catch, so an override cannot make a test read a column that is not there.

    Same @TestVisible-SOQL-string reason as corporateCurrencySoql below. Production never reassigns it. Deliberately unfiltered: a deactivated currency still has rows denominated in it, and dropping its rate would silently leave those totals in corporate currency.

    Per-transaction cache — one SOQL per transaction at most, and none at all in a single-currency org. @TestVisible so a test can seed a rate table or clear it between scenarios.

    Silent for the same reason corporateCurrency() is — see its note below. An empty map means "convert nothing", which leaves behaviour exactly as it was.

    The field names are parameters rather than literals so the rows-to-map step is reachable in the single-currency org this package is coverage-gated in: a test feeds it rows from a table every org has and names two fields that stand in for IsoCode/ConversionRate.

    Field name parameterised for the same coverage reason as toRateMap above.

    CurrencyType exists only in an org with Multiple Currencies enabled, so it is read through a dynamic query inside a try/catch — a typed reference would not COMPILE in a single-currency org and would therefore break the managed-package install everywhere. Same idiom, and the same reason, as CommunityService's Network queries. An accepted FFLib exception: a selector cannot be built for an SObject whose type may be absent.

    SYSTEM_MODE is deliberate. CurrencyType is org configuration, not customer data, and the guest user who most needs this answer has no access to it.

    The SOQL lives in a @TestVisible field rather than a literal for one reason: in a single-currency org the query throws on its first line, so the rows-to-code step below could never be exercised in the org this package is developed and coverage-gated in. A test repoints it at a table every org has and asserts the contract that matters — whatever goes wrong in here, the caller gets null and falls through to UserInfo rather than seeing an exception. Production code never reassigns it.

    Deliberately silent. AppLogger reads SettingsService.logLevel(), and this method sits on the resolution path every money-handling class calls — logging here risks recursion for no benefit, because the UserInfo fallback is always a valid answer.

    Method Returns
    forOrg(0 args) String
    forCampaign(1 arg) String
    forGateway(0 args) String
    forGateway(1 arg) String
    ratesByIso(0 args) Map<String, Decimal>
    toRecordCurrency(2 args) Decimal
    isoOf(1 arg) String
    isoCodesByRecordId(1 arg) Map<Id, String>

    CurrencyStampHelper#

    class · public inherited sharing

    shared/CurrencyStampHelper.cls

    Stamps the platform's standard CurrencyIsoCode field on a record the package is about to insert, so an org with Multi-Currency enabled reports the gift in the currency it was actually charged in rather than in whichever currency the inserting user happens to prefer.

    Stateless helper shared by every path that creates a record carrying a Currency-type amount: DonationService (the staging row and the finalized gift), EventRegistrationController and EventPurchaseService (ticket orders), and RecurringDonationService (the schedule and each installment Opportunity).

    Docs note

    WHY THIS EXISTS. The package has always written its own custom Opportunity.Currency_ISO_Code__c — what the gateway charged — and never touched the standard CurrencyIsoCode. In a single-currency org that is harmless, because CurrencyIsoCode does not exist. In a Multi-Currency org the platform interprets every Currency-type field IN the record's CurrencyIsoCode, and a record inserted without one takes the INSERTING USER's personal currency. Donations are inserted by StagingReconciliationBatch, so that was the batch user's currency — a value with no relationship to what Stripe charged. Every rollup, report and forecast was then wrong by the conversion rate, with no error raised and nothing in the UI to hint at it. Audit finding F-105.

    The two fields are kept, and they are not redundant. Currency_ISO_Code__c is the audit record of what the processor actually took; CurrencyIsoCode is what the platform reports in. They hold the same value today, and the custom field is the one that survives a future per-campaign-currency feature unchanged.

    NEVER reference CurrencyIsoCode with a typed expression. The field does not exist in a single-currency org, so opp.CurrencyIsoCode fails to COMPILE there — which for a managed package means the version will not install. Every access goes through SObject.put() with the name as a string, gated on the describe check below. This mirrors fflib_SObjectSelector's own CURRENCY_ISO_CODE_ENABLED guard.

    Test seams. UserInfo.isMultiCurrencyOrganization() cannot be stubbed and no scratch org is both single- and multi-currency, so the two environment facts this class depends on are each overridable. Tests set them to exercise the decision in codeToStamp() without needing a multi-currency org; applyTo() itself still refuses to write a field that is not really there, so an override can never make a test corrupt a record.

    The code is UPPER-CASED here and that is load-bearing, not cosmetic. Every stored code in this package is lower-case, because CurrencyResolver.forGateway() lower-cases it to satisfy Stripe, and that lower-case value is what lands in Donation_Staging__c.Currency_Code__c and Opportunity.Currency_ISO_Code__c. CurrencyIsoCode is a picklist of upper-case ISO-4217 codes, so passing 'usd' straight through would be rejected as an invalid value.

    NO GUARD against a currency the org has not activated, and since 2026-08-22 none is needed. The code stamped here originates from CurrencyResolver, which reads the org's own currency — the corporate currency in a multi-currency org, UserInfo.getDefaultCurrency() otherwise. A currency the org runs on is by definition one it can save, so INVALID_CURRENCY_ISO_CODE is now structurally unreachable on this path.

    It was reachable before. The code used to come from Settings__c.Default_Currency__c, an admin-typed value seeded to 'USD' at install and unrelated to the org's real currency, so an unactivated code took the donation path down at charge time. That hazard is what SetupController.currencyCheck() pre-flighted; both the field and the health check were deleted with the resolver change, because detecting a misconfiguration the package created for itself was never the fix. Do not reintroduce a settings-sourced currency here.

    PRESENT IS NOT THE SAME AS WRITABLE. On an SObject whose currency is DERIVED from a parent — OpportunityLineItem takes the parent Opportunity's, and the same holds for other line-item-shaped types — CurrencyIsoCode is present in the describe but rejects a write with "Field CurrencyIsoCode is not editable". Presence alone was the original guard and it was sufficient while the only callers stamped Opportunity, Donation_Staging__c and Recurring_Donation__c, all of which own their currency. It stops being sufficient the moment anything stamps a derived type, so the check is createability, not existence. Skipping is deliberately preferred to throwing: a derived record already reports in its parent's currency, which is the value this class would have written anyway, so there is nothing to correct and no reason to fail an insert over it.

    Method Returns
    applyTo(2 args) void

    DiagnosticLogController#

    class · public with sharing

    shared/DiagnosticLogController.cls

    DiagnosticLogController — backs the lexDiagnosticLogs tab (Diagnostic Logs).

    FFLib role: controller. Reads Log__c through ILogsSelector and writes nothing at all; the only other values it returns are the two Settings__c reads that decide whether the list can be trusted (the persisted log level, and how long entries survive before the retention purge).

    Docs note

    Split out of SetupController on 2026-08-15, in the same change that moved the "Recent diagnostic logs" list out of the Settings-console Logging panel and onto its own Lightning tab — the same treatment, and for the same reason, as DonationMonitorController the day before.

    Reading logs is troubleshooting: it happens when something has already gone wrong, repeatedly, and often by staff who have no business changing the org's settings. It could not stay on SetupController. That class is the single Apex entry point behind all 16 settings panels and is granted to Fundraising_Admin ONLY (its absence from Fundraising_User carries a "must not be added back" note saying exactly that), so reaching a log viewer through it would have meant handing out the whole console with it.

    The surface here is one read, with no caller-supplied filter, field list, or record id — every filter on the page is applied client-side over the rows already returned. So the new grant widens access to nothing beyond Log__c itself, which the permission sets that get this tab (Fundraising_Admin, Fundraising_ReadOnly) already read today.

    The panel it left behind (setLogging) keeps the log-level, debug-echo and retention SETTINGS plus the Transaction_Log__c error list — those are configuration, not diagnosis.

    Returns { rows, rowLimit, logLevel, retentionDays } rather than a bare list, because an empty log list is ambiguous on its own and the two settings are what disambiguate it. logLevel is the threshold AppLogger writes at: at OFF nothing is being recorded at all, and at the ERROR default the INFO/DEBUG entry an admin is hunting for was never written — so "no logs" means "logging is off", not "nothing went wrong". Without it the page would report a silent org as a healthy one.

    retentionDays is the other half: RetentionPurgeBatch deletes by CreatedDate, so a search for last quarter's incident can come back empty because the entries were purged, not because they never existed. The page states the window instead of letting the admin guess.

    Method Returns
    getRecentLogs(0 args) Map<String, Object> @AuraEnabled

    GuestAction#

    class · public abstract inherited sharing

    extends ControllerAction

    shared/GuestAction.cls

    GuestAction — base for every controller action an unauthenticated visitor can reach.

    ControllerAction's default hands the raw platform message back to the caller, which is the right default for an internal Lightning screen staffed by an admin: it makes a failure diagnosable without opening the log. On a public page it is an information leak. An uncaught fflib CRUD failure prints an internal object API name, a record id and an Apex stack trace straight onto the screen of whoever loaded the URL — no login required, and search engines and support-ticket screenshots carry it further.

    Extend this instead of ControllerAction whenever the action is reachable from a lightningCommunity__* bundle. AppException — the package's own, donor-worded validation ("Enter an amount greater than zero.") — is passed through unchanged, because that text exists precisely to be read by a donor. Everything else (DmlException, QueryException, fflib's DomainException, a null dereference) collapses to one neutral sentence. ControllerAction.run() has already written the real message and stack trace to AppLogger before this is called, so nothing is lost to whoever has to debug it.

    Validation errors added with addError() are NOT affected — they never travel this path, and "Amount is required" still reaches the donor verbatim.

    Docs note

    The guest counterpart to ControllerAction. Which controllers must use it is decided by the Fundraising_GuestDonor permission set: every class listed in its <classAccesses> is, by definition, callable by an unauthenticated visitor. UiConfigController is the one member of that list which does NOT extend this — see the rationale comment on the class itself. The passthrough is only as safe as the AppException messages behind it: an AppException raised on a guest-reachable path must be written for a donor and must not embed a record id or an internal API name, because it is printed verbatim.

    GuestThrottleService#

    class · public without sharing

    implements IGuestThrottleService

    shared/GuestThrottleService.cls

    GuestThrottleService — the rate limit in front of every guest-initiated checkout intent.

    FFLib role: Service. Read-only by construction — it counts through a Selector and writes nothing, so a throttled attempt leaves no trace of its own to be flooded in turn.

    The endpoints it guards are unauthenticated by design: anyone who can load a campaign page can ask the package to open a PaymentIntent. Two distinct abuses follow from that, and they need different keys, which is why there are two limits rather than one:

    • Card testing. A carder pushes a stolen list through one address, learns which numbers are live from the decline codes, and leaves the subscriber holding the authorization fees. The per-email limit is what makes that expensive, so it is keyed per campaign as well — a real donor who gives to two appeals in the same minute is not the target.
    • Staging-row flooding. The same attacker rotating addresses stays under every per-email ceiling forever. The per-campaign count is the circuit breaker for that: deliberately loose enough that a genuine surge (a matching hour, a broadcast mention) passes, tight enough that a script does not.

    without sharing, and this is load-bearing rather than copied habit. The counts must see every visitor's rows, not the caller's; the selector runs SYSTEM_MODE, but SYSTEM_MODE leaves record sharing to the class sharing keyword, so with sharing here would silently narrow both counts to whatever the guest user happens to own and the throttle would never fire. Nothing about another visitor's row leaves this class — both selector methods return a bare Integer.

    assertIntentAllowed's failure is explicit, unlike DonorPortalService's code-request cooldown, which is a silent no-op. That one stays silent because speaking would confirm an address is on file; the checkout path has no such oracle — the attempts being counted are the caller's own — so a donor who trips the limit is told, instead of watching a button do nothing.

    isPortalCodeRequestAllowed is the third bucket and the one exception to that: it guards the portal sign-in form, which is the same anti-enumeration surface as the cooldown, so it returns a Boolean the caller drops on the floor rather than throwing a message a visitor could read. Both of the checkout limits above still throw; a method here is loud or quiet according to whether the endpoint it guards can afford to speak, not according to a house style.

    Docs note

    Thresholds are Tier 1 settings (Settings__c, via SettingsService), editable by an admin in the Settings console without a package upgrade — a limit that can only be changed by shipping a new version is not a limit anyone can respond to an incident with. This is the package's own control, not a replacement for the gateway's. Stripe Radar sees signals no Salesforce-side count can (card fingerprints across merchants, device, network), and the two are complementary: Radar blocks the individual charge, this stops the flood from reaching the org at all. The admin-facing write-up is in the Stripe integration guide.

    Method Returns
    newInstance(0 args) [IGuestThrottleService](#apex-iguestthrottleservice)
    assertIntentAllowed(2 args) void
    calls: SettingsService.guestIntentMaxPerEmail(), SettingsService.guestIntentEmailWindowMinutes(), AppLogger.warn(), SettingsService.guestIntentMaxPerCampaign(), SettingsService.guestIntentCampaignWindowMinutes()
    isPortalCodeRequestAllowed(0 args) Boolean
    calls: SettingsService.portalCodeMaxPerHour()

    IGuestThrottleService#

    interface · public

    shared/IGuestThrottleService.cls

    LicenseService#

    class · public with sharing

    shared/LicenseService.cls

    Answers one question: does the running user hold a seat on this package's licence?

    Static utility (like DonorResolutionService), not an Application.Service binding — it holds no state beyond a per-transaction cache and has exactly one read-only method.

    Docs note

    Why this exists at all: Salesforce's per-seat licensing (LMA) caps how many users an admin can ASSIGN, and blocks unlicensed users from packaged Apex entry points "in most cases" — but packaged Lightning web components are NOT licence-enforced by the platform. Without an in-app check, an org could buy one seat and run every console tab for its whole staff. Salesforce's documented ISV pattern is UserInfo.isCurrentUserLicensed(namespace) in an Apex controller, with the component deciding what to render — which is exactly what this class feeds, through LicenseController.getLicenseStatus().

    FAIL OPEN, always. Every path that cannot positively prove "unlicensed" answers true: an org with no namespace (the dev scratch org, where the package is source-deployed rather than installed) is licensed; an exception from the platform call is licensed. The check exists to nudge honest orgs toward buying seats, and a licensing bug must degrade to "everything works" — never to a paying customer locked out of their own donation data. Guest/donor surfaces never consult this class at all: donors and event attendees never need a licence, and no guest controller may ever call it.

    Test seam for the final verdict (the SettingsService *Override idiom) — packaged tests must pass in both a licensed and an unlicensed org, so they pin the answer rather than depend on the org the suite happens to run in.

    Test seam for the namespace lookup: the dev org describes to an empty prefix, so forcing one here is the only way a test can reach the UserInfo call (and its fail-open catch — 'pledgivo' is not an installed licence in the dev org, so the platform call throws and the catch is exercised for real).

    Derived from a describe at runtime, never hardcoded — the same idiom as ControllerAction.unprefixed(): a namespaced org describes Settings__c as ns__Settings__c (two "__"), an unmanaged org as Settings__c (one). Returns the bare namespace ('pledgivo'), which is what UserInfo.isCurrentUserLicensed() takes.

    Method Returns
    isCurrentUserLicensed(0 args) Boolean

    QrCodeEncoder#

    class · public with sharing

    shared/QrCodeEncoder.cls

    QrCodeEncoder — encodes a short string as a QR symbol and emits it as inline SVG.

    Shared utility, not an FFLib layer: a pure function with no SObject, SOQL or DML surface. Byte mode, error-correction level M, versions 1-6 selected automatically.

    Docs note

    Written from scratch rather than pulled from a library because the package's CSP trusts no script CDN, and because a QR has to be generated somewhere the print sheet and the receipt email can both reach — which rules out encoding it in the browser.

    Output is monochrome SVG path data with the quiet zone baked into the viewBox.

    It carries no theme token and no color of its own on purpose: a themed QR is a QR that fails at the door, and a page that tightens padding would otherwise eat the quiet zone the scanner needs.

    Versions stop at 6 so no version-information block is required — a ticket reference is about a dozen characters, and version 6 at ECC M holds 108. Above capacity this throws rather than truncating: a truncated symbol still scans, just to the wrong ticket.

    Apex exposes no byte array, so UTF-8 bytes come out of a hex round-trip through Blob. Encoding the string's characters directly would corrupt any payload outside ASCII. The nibbles are looked up by hand because Integer.valueOf has no radix overload.

    Method Returns
    toSvg(1 arg) String
    toSvgPath(1 arg) String
    moduleCount(1 arg) Integer

    QueryCondition#

    class · public virtual with sharing

    shared/QueryCondition.cls

    Composable SOQL WHERE clause builder.

    Works with Database.queryWithBinds() (available API 57+) so binding variables are passed as a Map — no Apex scope variable tricks needed.

    Usage in a selector method:

    QueryCondition cond = QueryCondition.of('Status = :status', 'status', 'Active') .andWhere(QueryCondition.of('CampaignId = :cid', 'cid', campaignId));

    String soql = newQueryFactory().setCondition(cond.getClause()).toSOQL(); return (List<Opportunity>) Database.queryWithBinds( soql, cond.getBindings(), AccessLevel.USER_MODE );

    Concrete condition inner classes (recommended for reuse):

    private class ActiveCondition extends QueryCondition { ActiveCondition() { super('Status__c = :status', new Map<String,Object>{ 'status' => 'Active' }); } }

    private class ByCampaignCondition extends QueryCondition { ByCampaignCondition(Set<Id> ids) { super('CampaignId IN :cids', new Map<String,Object>{ 'cids' => ids }); } }

    // Compose: QueryCondition cond = new ActiveCondition().andWhere(new ByCampaignCondition(campaignIds));

    SecureTokens#

    class · public inherited sharing

    shared/SecureTokens.cls

    Cryptographically-random identifiers for the webhook-free donation flow.

    • uuid() → a random 36-char UUID-shaped id (correlation id embedded in Stripe metadata).
    • opaque(n) → an n-byte random hex string (unguessable staging capability token).

    Uses Crypto.generateAesKey (CSPRNG) — never Math.random (predictable) — so the values cannot be guessed or enumerated by a guest.

    TestDataFactory#

    class · public without sharing

    shared/TestDataFactory.cls

    Docs note

    Settings__c is a Hierarchy Custom Setting — createSettings() below calls Settings__c.getOrgDefaults() + upsert, never new Settings__c() + insert, or the org-default row silently fails to apply to every context that reads settings without an explicit Id. The Event_* factory helpers were merged in from the former P2PTestDataFactory on 2026-07-13 — there is no separate test factory for them anymore.

    Every fixture is stamped with the org's own currency before it is returned, exactly as the product does at every insert site (CurrencyStampHelper). Without this, a fixture inserted in a MULTI-currency org silently takes the RUNNING USER's personal currency, so a gift created with Amount = 100 is stored as 100 of whatever the test user prefers and reads back through the corporate-currency aggregates as 100 / rate. Thirty-five tests failed that way on a multi-currency org while passing on a single-currency one — a fixture defect wearing the costume of a product bug, and it masked the real one (audit finding F-125). No-op in a single-currency org, where the field does not exist.

    The two entry points below exist for test classes that build their fixtures inline rather than through this factory. An unstamped fixture silently takes the RUNNING USER'S currency, not the org's, so in a multi-currency org whose corporate currency differs from the test user's every SUM(Amount) aggregate comes back converted — a gift inserted as 500 USD aggregates as 41666.67 INR and an assertion written in face value fails for a reason that has nothing to do with the code under test. Routing an inline insert through stampAndInsert() pins the fixture to the corporate currency, which is what production gifts already carry (CurrencyStampHelper stamps every donation path). No-op in a single-currency org, where there is no CurrencyIsoCode field to write. Audit finding F-125.

    ThankYouController#

    class · public without sharing

    shared/ThankYouController.cls

    The routed thank-you page: one read covering both the donation and the event confirmation.

    WITHOUT SHARING: reached by a guest immediately after payment, holding either the short-lived staging access token or the unguessable Receipt_Token__c. Authorization is possession of a token — never Salesforce sharing, and never an Id supplied by the client.

    PII: returns the donor's first name, their email (so the page can confirm where the receipt went), and per seat the attendee display name, tier and check-in status. Never the attendee's Email__c — the page doesn't render it, so it must not cross the wire.

    Docs note

    This exists as its own controller rather than two client calls to ReceiptController and EventTicketsController because the page needs one round trip, and because the per-seat QR codes are generated server-side by QrCodeEncoder — the LWC never encodes anything. The page is a real Experience Cloud route (/thank-you) rather than a step inside the donation form specifically so a conversion produces its own pageview for analytics.

    Two payload builders, ONE key set. giftSummary reads the finalized Opportunity; pendingSummary reads the Donation_Staging__c row the donor's own checkout wrote, and returns the same keys with the finalizer-only ones null. That symmetry is deliberate: the finalizer runs on a five-minute cron, so a donor arriving on ?access= usually lands on the pending payload, and the page must render a real confirmation from it rather than a holding message. The null keys are what the page replaces with one quiet placeholder line.

    The page footer, resolved exactly as the donation page's band resolves it — same two CampaignService methods, same campaign-over-org fallback, same showContact gate. A donor who lands here has just come from that page; a footer that named a different support address or dropped the privacy link would read as a different site.

    Method Returns
    getThankYou(2 args) Map<String, Object> @AuraEnabled

    Async (Batch/Queueable/Schedulable)#

    RetentionPurgeBatch#

    class · public without sharing

    implements Database.Batchable<SObject>, Database.Stateful

    shared/async/RetentionPurgeBatch.cls

    Docs note

    Adding a value here needs a matching case in start() AND a Database.executeBatch line in RetentionPurgeScheduler — a target nothing schedules is a table that still grows forever. Adding one does NOT need the scheduler restructured to stay under the 5-concurrent-batch limit: past five, a submitted batch waits in the Apex Flex Queue as Holding and starts when a slot frees. See the note on RetentionPurgeScheduler.execute for the real ceiling.

    A fixed 7-day window (spec §4.5), independent of the org-configurable SettingsService.dataRetentionDays() the other targets use above. The Session_Hash__c unique index is why unbounded growth here bites harder than a plain insert-only log: every Active/Issued row this batch never reaches still occupies a slot in that index.

    Method Returns
    start(1 arg) Database.QueryLocator
    calls: SettingsService.dataRetentionDays(), AppLogger.info()
    execute(2 args) void
    finish(1 arg) void
    calls: AppLogger.info()

    RetentionPurgeScheduler#

    class · public without sharing

    implements Schedulable

    shared/async/RetentionPurgeScheduler.cls

    Docs note

    Five batches from one scheduled execution is safe, and a sixth would be too — the number to worry about is not five. Salesforce holds up to 5 batch jobs queued or active at once; a Database.executeBatch beyond that is NOT rejected, it goes into the Apex Flex Queue with status Holding and starts on its own as a slot frees. The flex queue holds 100, and only submitting past THAT throws LimitException. So the real ceiling on this method is 100 minus whatever the subscriber org's own jobs are already holding, which is why adding a RetentionPurgeBatch.Target here needs no rework — but also why nobody should read "five" as the limit and start batching targets together to stay under it. (Verified 2026-09-05 against the Apex Developer Guide: "Use Batch Apex", "Execution Governors and Limits" and the FlexQueue class reference.)

    Method Returns
    execute(1 arg) void

    shared/selector/ILogsSelector.cls

    LogsSelector#

    class · public with sharing

    extends fflib_SObjectSelector · implements ILogsSelector

    shared/selector/LogsSelector.cls

    Docs note

    Asymmetric with AppLogger by design: AppLogger inserts Log__c directly (without sharing, no UoW — see that class) so a log write never fails because of the calling user's access, but reads here go through the normal fflib selector (USER_MODE) because reading logs back — e.g. DiagnosticLogController behind the Diagnostic Logs tab — is a staff-facing action that should respect the running admin's FLS/sharing like any other read.

    That asymmetry is why the tab is granted only to permission sets holding viewAllRecords on Log__c: the object is Private and AppLogger owns each row as whoever ran the transaction, so a user without it sees only their own sessions — a partial list that reads as a clean org.

    Method Returns
    newInstance(0 args) [ILogsSelector](#apex-ilogsselector)
    getSObjectFieldList(0 args) List<Schema.SObjectField>
    getSObjectType(0 args) Schema.SObjectType
    selectById(1 arg) List<Log__c>
